Prandtl-Batchelor flows on an annulus
Mingwen Fei, Chen Gao, Zhiwu Lin, Tao Tao

Abstract
. For steady two-dimensional Navier-Stokes flows with a single eddy (i.e. nested closed streamlines) in a simply connected domain, Prandtl (1905) and Batchelor (1956) found that in the inviscid limit, the vorticity is constant inside the eddy. In this paper, we consider the generalized Prandtl-Batchelor theory for the forced steady Navier-Stokes equations on an annulus. First, we observe that in the limit of infinite Reynolds number, if forced steady Navier-Stokes solutions has nested closed streamlines on an annulus, then the inviscid limit is a rotating shear flow uniquely determined by the external force and boundary conditions. We call solutions of steady Navier-Stokes equations with the above property Prandtl-Batchelor flows. Then, by constructing higher order approximate solutions of the forced steady Navier-Stokes equations and establishing the validity of Prandtl boundary layer expansion, we give a rigorous proof of the existence of Prandtl-Batchelor flows on an annulus with the wall velocities slightly different from the rigid-rotations along the same direction.



1.
Introduction

The study of steady Navier-Stokes equations has a long history, starting with the classical paper of J.Leary
[
30
]
, in which Leray proved that the nonhomogeneous boundary problem of steady Navier-Stokes equations in a two-dimensional bounded domain
\Omega
with
C^{2}
smooth boundary
\partial\Omega=\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}\Gamma_{j}
has a solution under the assumption
\int_{\Gamma_{j}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{n}dl=0,j=1,\cdot\cdot\cdot,N
. V.Korobkov, K.Pileckas and R.Russo in
[
29
]
obtained the existence theorem under the assumption
\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{\Gamma_{j}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{n}dl=0
. However, the problem of inviscid limit of steady Navier-Stokes equations is still largely open. One of the main difficulties is that there are infinitely many solutions for the steady Euler equations in a closed region, which is very different from the time-dependent inviscid limit where the leading Euler flow is uniquely determined by the initial and boundary conditions. Thus, a selection principle must be found to obtain the physical solution. It is unknown how to choose the Euler flow except for some special case, for example,
Prandtl in
[
38
]
considered the steady motion of slightly viscous incompressible fluid in a simply-connected region. By integrating the steady Navier-Stokes equations along a closed streamline and letting the Reynolds number converge to infinity, Prandtl formally found that the vorticity of steady Euler flow must be constant in a region of nested closed streamlines (i.e. a single eddy). However, Prandtl didn’t give an approach to determine this constant. This important property
was rediscovered later by Batchelor (1956) in
[
1
]
. This class of result is now usually referred to as Prandtl-Batchelor theory and such laminar flows are called Prandtl-Batchelor flows in the
literature. Furthermore, for Prandtl-Batchelor flows on a circular disk, the Euler flow must be Couette flows
(ar,0)
and the
constant
a
was given in
[
1
,
47
]
by exactly solving steady the nonlinear Prandtl equations in the Von Mises transformation.
The formula to determine
a
is usually referred to as the Batchelor-Wood formula. Similar results were given by Feymann and Lagerstrom in
[
5
]
. However, for Prandtl-Batchelor flows on a more
general domain, it’s difficult to determine the limiting vorticity
constant and only approximate results were obtained in
[
4
,
5
,
28
,
42
,
43
,
47
]
. In addition, the Prandtl-Batchelor theory for forced steady Navier-Stokes equations has been considered by H.Okamoto in
[
34
]
on torus when the external force is proportional to viscous force, and derived a necessary condition for the limiting Euler flow. H.Okamoto in
[
35
]
studied the stationary Kolmogolov flows on two-dimensional torus by numerical experiments and found that under a certain condition, the Navier-Stokes flows are “nearly singular”(nonsmooth) for large Reynolds numbers.

The Prandtl-Batchelor theory has important applications in many studies involving
laminar flows with small viscosity, for example, the nonlinear critical
layer theory near shear flows in
[
31
]
. The Prandtl-Batchelor theory can also be applied to general two-dimensional advection
diffusion equation of a passive scalar field
\theta(x,y)

\displaystyle u\cdot\nabla\theta-R^{-1}\Delta\theta=0,

where
u(x,y)
is a given steady incompressible flow and

R^{-1}
is the diffusion coefficient. For example,
homogenization of potential vorticity in the ocean circulation theory

[
37
,
40
,
41
]
and flux expulsion in the
self-excited dynamo theory
[
32
,
33
,
46
]
.

Despite the importance of Prandtl-Batchelor theory and its wide applications, there
is relatively few mathematical works on this problem. Kim initiated a mathematical study of Prandtl-Batchelor flows on a
disk in a series of works

[
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
,
26
,
27
,
28
]
. In particular, when the boundary velocity is slightly different from a
constant, the well-posedness of the Prandtl equations under the Batchelor-Wood
condition was shown in
[
23
]
and some asymptotic study of the boundary
layer expansion was given in
[
22
]
. However, he didn’t prove
the convergence of the boundary layer expansion in the limit of infinite Reynolds number. Recently, by constructing higher order approximate solutions of the
Navier-Stokes equations and establishing the validity of Prandtl boundary layer expansion, we give a rigorous proof of the
existence of Prandtl-Batchelor flows on a disk with the wall velocity slightly
different from the rigid-rotation in
[
6
]
.

In this paper, we extend the Prandtl-Batchelor theory to the forced steady Navier-Stokes equations on an annulus. The limiting Euler flow in this case is selected in the following way. Under some suitable assumptions, if the solution of steady Navier-Stokes equations with nested closed streamlines converges to steady Euler flows, then the limiting Euler flow must be a rotating shear flow
(u_{e}(r),0)
satisfying (
1.12
). In fact, F.Hamel and N.Nadirashvili in
[
15
]
proved that if steady Euler flow with rigid wall boundary conditions in two-dimensional annulus domain has no stagnation point, then the flow is circular in the sense that the streamlines are concentric. By integrating the steady Navier-Stokes equations along a circle and letting the Reynolds number converge to infinity, we can show that the velocity
u_{e}(r)
and the external force
F_{u}
must satisfy the ODE in (
1.12
). And the Batchelor-Wood formula determines the boundary conditions for the velocity
u_{e}(r)
in (
1.12
). Combining the ODE and the boundary conditions, we obtain the system (
1.12
) for the Euler flow. We call the above selection the Prandtl-Batchelor theory since it describes which Euler flow is selected in the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. In the Appendix, we will give a proof of the above property under the following assumptions: for
sufficiently small viscosity i) the steady flows of Navier-Stokes equations have nested closed streamlines; ii) any
interior domain of the annulus is separated from the boundary layer uniformly for vanishing viscosity; iii) inside
the interior domain, steady Navier-Stokes solutions converge to a steady Euler solution in

C^{2}
. However, given a domain, boundary data and external force, it is hard
to understand the streamline structures of the steady
solutions of Navier-Stokes equations and control the boundary layer in a domain, hence it seems difficult to verify the above assumptions
rigorously. Following the argument of
[
6
]
, by constructing higher order approximate solutions and establishing the convergence of Prandtl boundary layer expansion, we construct a class of Prandtl-Batchelor flows to the steady Navier-Stokes equations on an annulus with both the wall velocities slightly different from the rigid-rotations along the same direction.

More precisely, we consider the forced steady Navier-Stokes equations in a two-dimensional annulus centered on zero
A:=B_{1}(0)\backslash B_{r_{0}}(0)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}+\nabla p^{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{2}\mathbf{F},\\[5.0pt]
\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}=0,\end{array}\right.

(1.1)

with rotating boundary conditions

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}\big|_{\partial B_{1}}=(\alpha+\eta f(\theta))\mathbf{t},\ \mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}\big|_{\partial B_{r_{0}}}=(\beta+\eta g(\theta))\mathbf{t},

(1.2)

where
\varepsilon^{2}>0
is reciprocal to Reynolds number,
0<r_{0}<1
,
\alpha>0,\beta>0
,
\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}
is the velocity,
p^{\varepsilon}
is the pressure,
\mathbf{F}
is the external force,
\eta
is a small number,
\mathbf{t}
is the unit tangential vector to
\partial B_{1}
or
\partial B_{r_{0}}
,
f(\theta),g(\theta)
are
2\pi
-periodic smooth functions.

Let
\mathbf{n}
be the unit normal to
\partial B_{1}
and

\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}=u^{\varepsilon}(\theta,r)\mathbf{t}+v^{\varepsilon}(\theta,r)\mathbf{n},\ \mathbf{F}=F_{u}(\theta,r)\mathbf{t}+F_{v}(\theta,r)\mathbf{n},

then (
1.1
) reads

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}u^{\varepsilon}u^{\varepsilon}_{\theta}+rv^{\varepsilon}u^{\varepsilon}_{r}+u^{\varepsilon}v^{\varepsilon}+p^{\varepsilon}_{\theta}-\varepsilon^{2}\big(\frac{u^{\varepsilon}_{\theta\theta}}{r}+ru^{\varepsilon}_{rr}+u^{\varepsilon}_{r}+\frac{2}{r}v^{\varepsilon}_{\theta}-\frac{u^{\varepsilon}}{r}\big)=\varepsilon^{2}rF_{u},\\[5.0pt]
u^{\varepsilon}v^{\varepsilon}_{\theta}+rv^{\varepsilon}v^{\varepsilon}_{r}-(u^{\varepsilon})^{2}+rp^{\varepsilon}_{r}-\varepsilon^{2}\big(\frac{v^{\varepsilon}_{\theta\theta}}{r}+rv^{\varepsilon}_{rr}+v^{\varepsilon}_{r}-\frac{2}{r}u^{\varepsilon}_{\theta}-\frac{v^{\varepsilon}}{r}\big)=\varepsilon^{2}rF_{v},\\[5.0pt]
u^{\varepsilon}_{\theta}+(rv^{\varepsilon})_{r}=0,\\[5.0pt]
u^{\varepsilon}(\theta,1)=\alpha+\eta f(\theta),~~v^{\varepsilon}(\theta,1)=0,\\[5.0pt]
u^{\varepsilon}(\theta,r_{0})=\beta+\eta g(\theta),~~v^{\varepsilon}(\theta,r_{0})=0,\end{array}\right.

where
(\theta,r)\in\Omega:=[0,2\pi]\times[r_{0},1]
. Formally, as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
,
we obtain the steady Euler equations for
(u_{e},v_{e})

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &u_{e}\partial_{\theta}u_{e}+v_{e}r\partial_{r}u_{e}+u_{e}v_{e}+\partial_{\theta}p_{e}=0,\\
&u_{e}\partial_{\theta}v_{e}+v_{e}r\partial_{r}v_{e}-(u_{e})^{2}+r\partial_{r}p_{e}=0,\\
&\partial_{\theta}u_{e}+\partial_{r}(rv_{e})=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.9)

We will show the existence of solution
(u^{\varepsilon},v^{\varepsilon})
to (
1
) which converges to a solution of steady Euler equations (
1.9
) satisfying (
1.12
). Following the argument of
[
6
]
, we first choose a steady Euler flow
(u_{e}(r),0)
with
u_{e}(r)
satisfying (
1.12
), then construct a class of Prandtl-Batchelor flows
(u^{\varepsilon},v^{\varepsilon})
to (
1
) by perturbing this steady Euler flow.
Generally, there is a mismatch between the tangential velocities of the Euler flow
u_{e}
and the prescribed Navier-Stokes flows
u^{\varepsilon}
. Due to the mismatch on the boundary, Prandtl in 1904 formally introduced the boundary layer theory to correct this mismatch. However, the justification of this formal boundary expansion is a challenging problem. The studies on steady Prandtl equations and linearized steady Prandtl equations can be found in
[
3
,
13
,
36
,
44
,
45
]
and the validity of Prandtl boundary layer expansion has also some important progresses, see
[
7
,
8
,
10
,
11
,
16
,
17
,
18
,
20
]
. Moreover, the stability in Sobolev space for some class shear flow of Prandtl type has been studied in
[
2
,
9
]
.

In the current work, we assume that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(\theta)d\theta=\int_{0}^{2\pi}g(\theta)d\theta=0,

(1.10)

and take the leading order Euler flow
(u_{e}(\theta,r),v_{e}(\theta,r))
to be the following shear flow

\displaystyle u_{e}(\theta,r)=u_{e}(r),\ v_{e}(\theta,r)=0,

(1.11)

where
u_{e}(r)
satisfies the second order ordinary differential equations

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &u^{\prime\prime}_{e}(r)+\frac{u^{\prime}_{e}(r)}{r}-\frac{u_{e}(r)}{r^{2}}=-\bar{F}_{u}(r),\\
&u_{e}(1)=\Big(\alpha^{2}+\frac{\eta^{2}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f^{2}(\theta)d\theta\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}},\\
&u_{e}(r_{0})=\Big(\beta^{2}+\frac{\eta^{2}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}g^{2}(\theta)d\theta\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}},\end{aligned}\right.

(1.12)

where
\bar{F}_{u}(r)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}F_{u}(\theta,r)d\theta,r\in[r_{0},1]
.
There is an important basis for this choice—the generalized Prandtl-Batchelor theory, see Appendix C. This theory shows that if the Euler flow is rotating shear
u_{e}(r)\vec{e}_{\theta}
in the vanishing viscosity limit, then
u_{e}(r)
must satisfy the second order ordinary differential equation in (
1.12
), and the Batchelor-Wood formula in Lemma
2.1
gives the boundary conditions for
u_{e}(r)
in (
1.12
).
Moreover, the solvability of linearized second order Euler equations in the matched asymptotic expansion also makes
u_{e}(r)
necessary to be this form, see Remark 2.1.

Remark 1.1
.

If the external force
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{0}
, then
u_{e}(r)=ar+\frac{b}{r}
(
a,b
can be uniquely determined by the boundary condition in (
1.12
)) is the Couette-Taylor flow and the associated vorticity is constant
2a
. If the external force
\mathbf{F}=\frac{2c}{r}\mathbf{e}_{\theta}
, then
u_{e}(r)=ar+\frac{b}{r}+cr\ln r
and the associated vorticity is
2a+c+2c\ln r
(
a,b
can be uniquely determined by the boundary condition in (
1.12
)).

Next we introduce the steady Prandtl equations near
r=1

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(u_{e}(1)+u_{p}^{(0)}\big)\partial_{\theta}u_{p}^{(0)}+\big(v_{p}^{(1)}-v_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,0)\big)\partial_{Y}u_{p}^{(0)}-\partial_{YY}u_{p}^{(0)}=0,\\[5.0pt]
\partial_{\theta}u_{p}^{(0)}+\partial_{Y}v_{p}^{(1)}=0,\\[5.0pt]
u_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Y)=u_{p}^{(0)}(\theta+2\pi,Y),\ v_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Y)=v_{p}^{(0)}(\theta+2\pi,Y),\\[5.0pt]
u_{p}^{(0)}\big|_{Y=0}=\alpha+\eta f(\theta)-u_{e}(1),\ \lim\limits_{Y\rightarrow-\infty}u_{p}^{(0)}=\lim\limits_{Y\rightarrow-\infty}v_{p}^{(1)}=0\end{array}\right.

and the steady Prandtl equations near
r=r_{0}

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(u_{e}(r_{0})+\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}\big)\partial_{\theta}\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}+\big(\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)}-\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,0)\big)r_{0}\partial_{Z}\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}-r_{0}\partial_{ZZ}\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}=0,\\[5.0pt]
\partial_{\theta}\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}+r_{0}\partial_{Z}\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)}=0,\\[5.0pt]
\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Z)=\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta+2\pi,Z),\ \hat{v}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Z)=\hat{v}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta+2\pi,Z),\\[5.0pt]
\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}\big|_{Z=0}=\beta+\eta g(\theta)-u_{e}(r_{0}),\ \lim\limits_{Z\rightarrow+\infty}\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}=\lim\limits_{Z\rightarrow+\infty}\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)}=0,\end{array}\right.

where
Y=\frac{r-1}{\varepsilon}
and
Z=\frac{r-r_{0}}{\varepsilon}
.
The above steady Prandtl equations will be derived by matched asymptotic expansions and their solvability will be studied in the next section.

Now our main theorem is stated as follows.

Theorem 1.2
.

Assume that
f(\theta),g(\theta),F_{u}(\theta,r),F_{v}(\theta,r)
are smooth functions satisfying (
1.10
) and the solution
u_{e}(r)
of (
1.12
) has a positive lower bound, then there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0,\eta_{0}>0,C>0
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}),\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
, the Navier-Stokes equations (
1
) have a solution
(u^{\varepsilon}(\theta,r),v^{\varepsilon}(\theta,r))
which satisfies

\displaystyle\Big\|u^{\varepsilon}(\theta,r)-u_{e}(r)-u_{p}^{(0)}\Big(\theta,\frac{r-1}{\varepsilon}\Big)-\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}\Big(\theta,\frac{r-r_{0}}{\varepsilon}\Big)\Big\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C\varepsilon,

\displaystyle\|v^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C\varepsilon,

where
(u_{e}(r),0)
is the shear flow satisfying (
1.12
),
(u_{p}^{(0)},v_{p}^{(1)})
is the solution of steady Prandtl equations (
1
) and
(\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)},\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)})
is the solution of steady Prandtl equations (
1
).

Moreover, for any
r_{0}<r_{1}<r_{2}<1
, there holds

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\|w^{\varepsilon}-w_{e}(r)\|_{L^{\infty}(A_{r_{1},r_{2}})}=0,

where
w^{\varepsilon}(\theta,r)
is the vorticity of
(u^{\varepsilon}(\theta,r),v^{\varepsilon}(\theta,r))
,
w_{e}(r)
is the vorticity of
(u_{e}(r),0)
and
A_{r_{1},r_{2}}=B_{r_{2}}(0)\backslash B_{r_{1}}(0)
.

Remark 1.3
.

If
\bar{F}_{u}(r)\geq 0,\ \forall r\in[r_{0},1]
, then the solution
u_{e}(r)
of (
1.12
) has a positive lower bound by maximum principle.

Remark 1.4
.

We note that the positivity assumption of
u_{e}(r)
in Theorem
1.2
is a sharp condition for structural stability of shear flow
(u_{e}(r),0)
. The structural stability of rotating shear flow
(u_{e}(r),0)
means that

•

The rotating shear flow
(u_{e}(r),0)
is a solution to (
1
) with external force
(F_{u},F_{v})=(u^{\prime\prime}_{e}(r)+\frac{u^{\prime}_{e}(r)}{r}-\frac{u_{e}(r)}{r^{2}},0)
and constant boundary conditions.

•

Under the small perturbation on external force and boundary conditions, the solution to (
1
) is still nearly rotating shear flow(“rotating shear flow”+“small error term”).

On the one hand, Theorem
1.2
shows that if
u_{e}(r)
has a positive lower bound, then the shear flow
(u_{e}(r),0)
is structurally stable. On the other hand, if
u_{e}(r)
has a degenerate point, small perturbations on boundary and force will cause hyperbolic singularities for the Euler flow, then the “cat eye” structure may appear, thus we can’t expect structural stability. Hence, in this sense, the positivity of
u_{e}(r)
is a sharp condition for structural stability of shear flow
(u_{e}(r),0)
and also for the existence of PB flow.

Remark 1.5
.

We write
\Omega(r)=\frac{u_{e}(r)}{r}
as the angular velocity of the Euler flow. From the Rayleigh equation for rotating shear flow

(\Omega-c)\Big(D_{\ast}D-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{r^{2}}\Big)\phi-\frac{(rD^{2}\Omega+3D\Omega)\phi}{r}=0,~~D=\frac{d}{dr},D_{\ast}=\frac{d}{dr}+\frac{1}{r},

it is easy to deduce that if the shear flow
(u_{e}(r),0)
is dynamically unstable for the Euler equations,
(u_{e}(r)+Cr,0)
is also dynamically unstable(Lyapunov unstable) for any constant
C
. Furthermore, it is expected that if the shear flow
(u_{e}(r),0)
is dynamically unstable for the Euler equations, it is also dynamically unstable for the Navier-Stokes equations with small viscosity. For any dynamically unstable shear flow
(u_{e}(r),0)
, we choose
C
such that
u_{e}(r)+Cr
has positive below bound. Thus the shear flow
(u_{e}(r)+Cr,0)
is dynamically unstable, but structurally stable from Theorem
1.2
. Hence, dynamical stability and structural stability are totally separate.

Remark 1.6
.

The condition
\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(\theta)d\theta=\int_{0}^{2\pi}g(\theta)d\theta=0
can be dropped due to the fact

\alpha+\eta f(\theta)=\alpha+\frac{\eta}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(\theta)d\theta+\eta\tilde{f}(\theta),

where
\int_{0}^{2\pi}\tilde{f}(\theta)d\theta=0.
Moreover, the smoothness of
f(\theta),g(\theta),F_{u}(\theta,r),F_{v}(\theta,r)
can be relaxed, but we don’t pursue this issue in this manuscript.

Remark 1.7
.

We believe that our current method can be applicable to the channel flow. More precisely, we consider the two-dimensional forced steady Navier-Stokes equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}+\nabla p^{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{2}\mathbf{F},~~(x,y)\in[0,2\pi]\times[0,1]\\[5.0pt]
\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}=0,~~(x,y)\in[0,2\pi]\times[0,1]\\[5.0pt]
\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}(x+2\pi,y)=\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}(x,y),~~y\in[0,1]\\[5.0pt]
\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}(x,0)=(\alpha+\eta f(x))\vec{\mathbf{e}}_{1},\quad\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}(x,0)=(\beta+\eta g(x))\vec{\mathbf{e}}_{1},\end{array}\right.

(1.22)

where
\alpha>0,\beta>0,\vec{\mathbf{e}}_{1}=(1,0)^{T}
,
f(x),g(x)
are
2\pi
-periodic smooth functions and
\eta
is a small number. In Appendix C, we proved that under some assumption the inviscid limit of the solutions
\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}
to (
1.22
) must satisfy
u^{\prime\prime}_{e}(y)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}F_{1}(x,y)dx,\ \forall y\in[0,1]
, where
F_{1}
is the first component of
\mathbf{F}
. We believe that following the argument of the current paper, when the boundary conditions are slightly different with uniform motion, we can construct solutions
\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}
to (
1.22
) which convergence to the shear flow
(u_{e}(y),0)^{T}
when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.

Now we present a sketch of the proof and some key ideas.

Step 1: Construction of the approximate solution.
We construct an approximate solution
(u^{a},v^{a})
by matched asymptotic expansion. The leading order of
(u^{a},v^{a})
is

\displaystyle\Big(u_{e}(r)+u_{p}^{(0)}\Big(\theta,\frac{r-1}{\varepsilon}\Big)+\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}\Big(\theta,\frac{r-r_{0}}{\varepsilon}\Big),0\Big).

The details of constructing the approximate solution will be given in Section 2. After the construction of the approximate solution, we derive the equations (
3.1
) for the error
(u,v):=(u^{\varepsilon}-u^{a},v^{\varepsilon}-v^{a})
.
Notice that the nonlinear term can be easily handled by higher order approximation, hence we only need to consider the linearized error equations

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}u^{a}u_{\theta}+uu^{a}_{\theta}+v^{a}ru_{r}+vru^{a}_{r}+v^{a}u+vu^{a}+p_{\theta}-\varepsilon^{2}\big(ru_{rr}+\frac{u_{\theta\theta}}{r}+2\frac{v_{\theta}}{r}+u_{r}-\frac{u}{r}\big)=F_{1},\\[5.0pt]
u^{a}v_{\theta}+uv^{a}_{\theta}+v^{a}rv_{r}+vrv^{a}_{r}-2uu^{a}+rp_{r}-\varepsilon^{2}\big(rv_{rr}+\frac{v_{\theta\theta}}{r}-2\frac{u_{\theta}}{r}+v_{r}-\frac{v}{r}\big)=F_{2},\\[5.0pt]
u_{\theta}+(rv)_{r}=0,\\[5.0pt]
u(\theta+2\pi,r)=u(\theta,r),\ v(\theta+2\pi,r)=v(\theta,r),\\[5.0pt]
u(\theta,1)=0,\ v(\theta,1)=0,\\[5.0pt]
u(\theta,r_{0})=0,\ v(\theta,r_{0})=0.\end{array}\right.

Step 2: Linear stability estimate for (
1
).

Equations (
1
) are the linearized Navier-Stokes equations around the approximate solution
(u^{a},v^{a})
. For simply, we assume
(u^{a},v^{a})\approx\Big(u_{e}(r)+u_{p}^{(0)}\big(\theta,\frac{r-1}{\varepsilon}\big),0\Big)
because we can deal with the boundary layer profile near
r=r_{0}
in the similar way. Since
|u^{a}_{\theta}|=|\partial_{\theta}u^{(0)}_{p}|\lesssim\eta
, the leading order of the above system can be simplified as

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}u^{a}u_{\theta}+vru^{a}_{r}+vu^{a}+p_{\theta}-\varepsilon^{2}\big(ru_{rr}+\frac{u_{\theta\theta}}{r}+2\frac{v_{\theta}}{r}+u_{r}-\frac{u}{r}\big)=F_{1},\\[5.0pt]
u^{a}v_{\theta}-2uu^{a}+rp_{r}-\varepsilon^{2}\big(rv_{rr}+\frac{v_{\theta\theta}}{r}-2\frac{u_{\theta}}{r}+v_{r}-\frac{v}{r}\big)=F_{2},\\[5.0pt]
u_{\theta}+(rv)_{r}=0,\\[5.0pt]
u(\theta+2\pi,r)=u(\theta,r),\ v(\theta+2\pi,r)=v(\theta,r),\\[5.0pt]
u(\theta,1)=0,\ v(\theta,1)=0,\\[5.0pt]
u(\theta,r_{0})=0,\ v(\theta,r_{0})=0,\end{array}\right.

where
u^{a}
can be regarded as
u_{e}(r)+u_{p}^{(0)}\big(\theta,\frac{r-1}{\varepsilon}\big)
.

The linear stability estimate consists of a basic energy estimate and positivity estimate. In fact, it is easy to know that the basic energy estimate is not good enough for closing the estimate because
\varepsilon
is small. The key point is the following observation which gives the important positivity estimate:
u^{a}
is strictly positive, we should make use of the terms
u^{a}u_{\theta}
and
u^{a}v_{\theta}
to obtain a positive quantity from the convective term. To do this, we choose
\big(-\big(\frac{r^{2}v}{u^{a}}\big)_{r},\big(\frac{rv}{u^{a}}\big)_{\theta}\big)
as the multiplier, the pressure is eliminated due to the divergence-free condition and the diffusion term is dominated easily due to the small viscosity
\varepsilon^{2}
. Here we only give a sketch of deriving the key positive quantity as follows. Firstly one has

\displaystyle-\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(u^{a}u_{\theta}+vru^{a}_{r}+vu^{a}\big)\Big(\frac{r^{2}v}{u^{a}}\Big)_{r}d\theta dr

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(u^{a}v_{\theta}-2uu^{a}\big)\Big(\frac{rv}{u^{a}}\Big)_{\theta}d\theta dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(u^{a}u_{\theta}+vru^{a}_{r}+vu^{a}\big)\Big(\frac{ru_{\theta}}{u^{a}}-rv\Big(\frac{r}{u^{a}}\Big)_{r}\Big)d\theta dr

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(u^{a}v_{\theta}-2uu^{a}\big)\Big(\frac{rv_{\theta}}{u^{a}}+rv\Big(\frac{1}{u^{a}}\Big)_{\theta}\Big)d\theta dr

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(u^{a}u_{\theta}+vru^{a}_{r}+vu^{a}\big)\Big(\frac{ru_{\theta}}{u^{a}}-rv\Big(\frac{r}{u^{a}}\Big)_{r}\Big)d\theta dr

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(u^{a}v_{\theta}-2uu^{a}\big)\Big(\frac{rv_{\theta}}{u^{a}}\Big)d\theta dr

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(ru^{2}_{\theta}+rv^{2}_{\theta}\big)d\theta dr+\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{1}{u^{a}}\Big(ru^{a}_{rr}+u^{a}_{r}-\frac{u^{a}}{r}\Big)(rv)^{2}d\theta dr,

here the details of obtaining the last step will be given in subsection
3.2
.

Since
u^{a}\approx u_{e}(r)+u^{(0)}_{p}(\theta,\frac{r-1}{\varepsilon})
, the boundary layer profile
u^{(0)}_{p}(\theta,Y)
is small in the Prandtl variable
Y=\frac{r-1}{\varepsilon}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{1}{u^{a}}\Big(r\partial^{2}_{r}u^{(0)}_{p}+\partial_{r}u^{(0)}_{p}-\frac{u^{(0)}_{p}}{r}\Big)(rv)^{2}d\theta dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{1}{u^{a}}\Big(rY^{2}\partial^{2}_{Y}u^{(0)}_{p}+\varepsilon Y^{2}\partial_{Y}u^{(0)}_{p}-\frac{(\varepsilon Y)^{2}u^{(0)}_{p}}{r}\Big)\Big(\frac{rv}{r-1}\Big)^{2}d\theta dr

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\eta\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big(\frac{rv}{r-1}\Big)^{2}d\theta dr\lesssim\eta\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big((rv)_{r}\big)^{2}d\theta dr\lesssim\eta\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}u_{\theta}^{2}d\theta dr,

where we have used the Hardy inequality.

Moreover, if the Euler flow is Taylor-Coutte flow, that is
u_{e}(r)=ar+\frac{b}{r}
, then
ru^{\prime\prime}_{e}+u^{\prime}_{e}-\frac{u_{e}}{r}=0
. So

\displaystyle\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(ru^{2}_{\theta}+rv^{2}_{\theta}\big)d\theta dr+\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{1}{u^{a}}\Big(ru^{a}_{rr}+u^{a}_{r}-\frac{u^{a}}{r}\Big)(rv)^{2}d\theta dr

\displaystyle\geq(1-C\eta)\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(ru^{2}_{\theta}+rv^{2}_{\theta}\big)d\theta dr,

where the constant
C
is independent of
\eta
. So we obtain a positive quantity
\|(u_{\theta},v_{\theta})\|_{2}
. When
u_{e}
is strictly positive, we can also obtain the same estimate which will be given in the text.

However, the positive quantity
\|(u_{\theta},v_{\theta})\|_{2}
don’t contain zero-frequency of
(u,v)
which is needed to obtain the
L^{\infty}
estimate for the error
(u,v)
. Notice that
\int_{0}^{2\pi}v(\theta,r)d\theta=0
because of the divergence-free condition and the boundary conditions, we can dominate
\|v\|_{2}
by
\|v_{\theta}\|_{2}
using the Poincaré inequality. However
u_{0}(r):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}u(\theta,r)d\theta\neq 0
, we need to obtain the estimate of
|u_{0}(r)|
from the basic energy estimate.

Now we give a sketch of the basic energy estimate. Choose
u_{0}
as a multiplier to the first equation in (
1
). The diffusion term is

\displaystyle\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}-\varepsilon^{2}\Big(ru_{rr}+\frac{u_{\theta\theta}}{r}+2\frac{v_{\theta}}{r}+u_{r}-\frac{u}{r}\Big)u_{0}(r)d\theta dr=\varepsilon^{2}\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big(r|u^{\prime}_{0}|^{2}+\frac{u_{0}^{2}}{r}\Big)d\theta dr.

We decompose the approximate solution as
u^{a}\approx u_{e}(r)+u^{(0)}_{p}(\theta,\frac{r-1}{\varepsilon})
. Due to the Euler flow is radial, we deduce that the following quantity vanishes

\displaystyle\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(u_{e}u_{\theta}+vru_{er}+vu_{e}+p_{\theta}\big)u_{0}d\theta dr=0,

where we used
\int_{0}^{2\pi}v(\theta,r)d\theta=0
. Moreover, the Prandtl part can be handled by the Hardy inequality as above

\displaystyle\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(u_{p}^{(0)}u_{\theta}+vr\partial_{r}u_{p}^{(0)}+vu_{p}^{(0)}\big)u_{0}d\theta dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\varepsilon\big(Yu_{p}^{(0)}u_{\theta}+\frac{vr}{r-1}Y^{2}\partial_{Y}u_{p}^{(0)}+vYu_{p}^{(0)}\big)\frac{u_{0}}{r-1}d\theta dr\lesssim\eta\varepsilon\|(u_{\theta},v_{\theta})\|_{2}\|u^{\prime}_{0}\|_{2}.

Thus, we obtain that
\varepsilon^{2}\|u^{\prime}_{0}\|_{2}\lesssim\|(u_{\theta},v_{\theta})\|_{2}
. One can see Lemma
3.2
for the basic energy estimate.

Combining the positivity estimate and basic energy estimate, we obtain the linear stability of equations (
1
).

Step 3:
L^{\infty}
estimate for (
1
).
To close the nonlinearity, we need the
L^{\infty}
estimate. Using the anisotropic Sobolev embedding, we only need to estimate
\|(u_{r\theta},v_{r\theta})\|_{2}
which can be obtained by studying the Stokes equations.

Finally, combining the linear stability estimate and
L^{\infty}
estimate, we establish the well-posedness of the error equations by contraction mapping theorem.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we construct the approximate solution by the matched asymptotic expansion method. In Section 3, we derive the error equations and establish the linear stability estimate which consists of the basic energy estimate and positivity estimate. In Section 4, we firstly establish the “
H^{2}
estimate” for a Stokes system, then obtain
L^{\infty}
estimate by the anisotropic Sobolev embedding, finally complete the proof of Theorem
1.2
by combining the linear stability estimate and
L^{\infty}
estimate.

2.
Construction of approximate solutions

In this section, we construct an approximate solution of the Navier-Stokes equations (
1
) by the matched asymptotic expansion.

2.1.
Euler expansions

Away from the boundary, we make the following formal expansions

\displaystyle u^{\varepsilon}(\theta,r)=u_{e}^{(0)}(\theta,r)+\varepsilon u_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r)+\text{h.o.t.},

\displaystyle v^{\varepsilon}(\theta,r)=v_{e}^{(0)}(\theta,r)+\varepsilon v_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r)+\text{h.o.t.},

\displaystyle p^{\varepsilon}(\theta,r)=p_{e}^{(0)}(\theta,r)+\varepsilon p_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r)+\text{h.o.t.},

here and in what follows, “h.o.t.” means higher order terms.

2.1.1.
Equations for
(u_{e}^{(0)},v_{e}^{(0)},p_{e}^{(0)})

By substituting the above expansions into (
1
) and collecting the
\varepsilon
-zeroth order terms, we deduce that
(u_{e}^{(0)},v_{e}^{(0)},p_{e}^{(0)})
satisfies the following steady nonlinear Euler equations

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}u_{e}^{(0)}\partial_{\theta}u_{e}^{(0)}+rv_{e}^{(0)}\partial_{r}u_{e}^{(0)}+u_{e}^{(0)}v_{e}^{(0)}+\partial_{\theta}p_{e}^{(0)}=0,\\[7.0pt]
u_{e}^{(0)}\partial_{\theta}v_{e}^{(0)}+rv_{e}^{(0)}\partial_{r}v_{e}^{(0)}-(u_{e}^{(0)})^{2}+r\partial_{r}p_{e}^{(0)}=0,\\[7.0pt]
\partial_{\theta}u_{e}^{(0)}+\partial_{r}(rv_{e}^{(0)})=0.\end{array}\right.

In our current work, we assume that the leading order Euler flow
(u_{e}^{(0)},v_{e}^{(0)})
is the shear flow in (
1.11
). Then equations (
2.1.1
) lead to

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}p_{e}^{(0)}(\theta,r)=0,\ \partial_{r}p_{e}^{(0)}(\theta,r)=\frac{1}{r}u_{e}^{2}.

Thus we can write

\displaystyle p_{e}^{(0)}(\theta,r)=p_{e}(r),\ p^{\prime}_{e}(r)=\frac{1}{r}u_{e}^{2}(r).

(2.5)

2.1.2.
Equations for
(u_{e}^{(1)},v_{e}^{(1)},p_{e}^{(1)})

By collecting the
\varepsilon
-order terms, we deduce that
(u_{e}^{(1)},v_{e}^{(1)},p_{e}^{(1)})
satisfies the following linearized Euler equations in
\Omega

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}u_{e}(r)\partial_{\theta}u_{e}^{(1)}+rv_{e}^{(1)}u_{e}^{\prime}(r)+u_{e}(r)v_{e}^{(1)}+\partial_{\theta}p_{e}^{(1)}=0,\\[5.0pt]
u_{e}(r)\partial_{\theta}v_{e}^{(1)}-2u_{e}(r)u_{e}^{(1)}+r\partial_{r}p_{e}^{(1)}=0,\\[5.0pt]
\partial_{\theta}u_{e}^{(1)}+\partial_{r}(rv_{e}^{(1)})=0,\end{array}\right.

which are equipped with the boundary conditions

\displaystyle v_{e}^{(1)}|_{r=1}=-v_{p}^{(1)}|_{Y=0},\ v_{e}^{(1)}|_{r=r_{0}}=-\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)}|_{Z=0},\ v_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r)=v_{e}^{(1)}(\theta+2\pi,r),

(2.10)

where
v_{p}^{(1)},\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)}
is defined in (
2.2.2
) and (
2.3.2
) respectively in next subsection.

2.2.
Prandtl expansions near
\partial B_{1}

We introduce the scaled variable
Y=\frac{r-1}{\varepsilon}\in(-\infty,0]
and make the following Prandtl expansions near
\partial B_{1}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &u^{\varepsilon}=u_{e}^{(0)}(\theta,r)+u_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Y)+\varepsilon\big[u_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r)+u_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Y)\big]+\varepsilon^{2}\big[u_{e}^{(2)}(\theta,r)+u_{p}^{(2)}(\theta,Y)\big]+\text{h.o.t.},\\[5.0pt]
&v^{\varepsilon}=v_{e}^{(0)}(\theta,r)+v_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Y)+\varepsilon\big[v_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r)+v_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Y)\big]+\varepsilon^{2}\big[v_{e}^{(2)}(\theta,r)+v_{p}^{(2)}(\theta,Y)\big]+\text{h.o.t.},\\[5.0pt]
&p^{\varepsilon}=p_{e}^{(0)}(\theta,r)+p_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Y)+\varepsilon\big[p_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r)+p_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Y)\big]+\varepsilon\big[p_{e}^{(2)}(\theta,r)+p_{p}^{(2)}(\theta,Y)\big]+\text{h.o.t.},\end{aligned}

(2.11)

where as
Y\rightarrow-\infty

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}^{l}\partial_{Y}^{m}v_{p}^{(i)}(\theta,Y)\rightarrow 0,\ \partial_{\theta}^{l}\partial_{Y}^{m}p_{p}^{(i)}(\theta,Y)\rightarrow 0,

(2.12)

here
l,m\geq 0,i=0,1,\cdots,
and satisfy the following boundary conditions

\displaystyle u_{e}^{(0)}(\theta,1)+u_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,0)=\alpha+\eta f(\theta),\ u_{e}^{(i)}(\theta,1)+u_{p}^{(i)}(\theta,0)=0,\ i\geq 1,

\displaystyle v_{e}^{(i)}(\theta,1)+v_{p}^{(i)}(\theta,0)=0,i\geq 0,\ \lim_{Y\rightarrow-\infty}(u_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Y),\partial_{Y}u_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Y))=(0,0).

2.2.1.
Equations for
(v_{p}^{(0)},p_{p}^{(0)})

By substituting the above expansions into (
1
) and collecting the
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
order terms, we get

\displaystyle\partial_{Y}v_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Y)=0,\ \partial_{Y}p_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Y)=0,

which together with (
2.12
) implies

\displaystyle v_{p}^{(0)}=0,\ p_{p}^{(0)}=0.

2.2.2.
Equations for
(u_{p}^{(0)},v_{p}^{(1)},p_{p}^{(1)})

By substituting the above expansions into (
1
) and collecting the
\varepsilon
-zeroth order terms, we obtain
the following steady Prandtl equations for
(u_{p}^{(0)},v_{p}^{(1)})

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(u_{e}(1)+u_{p}^{(0)}\big)\partial_{\theta}u_{p}^{(0)}+\big(v_{p}^{(1)}-v_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,0)\big)\partial_{Y}u_{p}^{(0)}-\partial_{YY}u_{p}^{(0)}=0,\\[5.0pt]
\partial_{\theta}u_{p}^{(0)}+\partial_{Y}v_{p}^{(1)}=0,\\[5.0pt]
u_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Y)=u_{p}^{(0)}(\theta+2\pi,Y),\ v_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Y)=v_{p}^{(0)}(\theta+2\pi,Y),\\[5.0pt]
u_{p}^{(0)}\big|_{Y=0}=\alpha+\eta f(\theta)-u_{e}(1),\ \lim\limits_{Y\rightarrow-\infty}u_{p}^{(0)}=\lim\limits_{Y\rightarrow-\infty}v_{p}^{(1)}=0\end{array}\right.

and the pressure
p_{p}^{(1)}
satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{Y}p_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Y)=(u_{p}^{(0)})^{2}(\theta,Y)+2u_{e}(1)u_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Y),\ \lim_{Y\rightarrow-\infty}p_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Y)=0.

(2.18)

2.2.3.
Equations for
(u_{p}^{(1)},v_{p}^{(2)})

By substituting the above expansions into the first and third equation of (
1
) and collecting the
\varepsilon
-order terms, we obtain
the following linearized steady Prandtl equations for
(u_{p}^{(1)},v_{p}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(u_{e}(1)+u_{p}^{(0)}\big)\partial_{\theta}u_{p}^{(1)}+\big(v_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,1)+v_{p}^{(1)}\big)\partial_{Y}u_{p}^{(1)}+(v_{p}^{(2)}-v_{p}^{(2)}(\theta,0))\partial_{Y}u_{p}^{(0)}\\[5.0pt]
\quad\quad\quad\quad+u_{p}^{(1)}\partial_{\theta}u_{p}^{(0)}-\partial_{YY}u_{p}^{(1)}=f_{1}(\theta,Y),\\[5.0pt]
\partial_{\theta}u_{p}^{(1)}+\partial_{Y}v_{p}^{(2)}+\partial_{Y}(Yv_{p}^{(1)})=0,\\[5.0pt]
u_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Y)=u_{p}^{(1)}(\theta+2\pi,Y),\ v_{p}^{(2)}(\theta,Y)=v_{p}^{(2)}(\theta+2\pi,Y),\\[5.0pt]
u_{p}^{(1)}\big|_{Y=0}=-u_{e}^{(1)}\big|_{r=1},\ \lim\limits_{Y\rightarrow-\infty}\partial_{Y}u_{p}^{(1)}=\lim\limits_{Y\rightarrow-\infty}v_{p}^{(2)}=0\end{array}\right.

and

\displaystyle f_{1}(\theta,Y)=

\displaystyle-\partial_{\theta}p_{p}^{(1)}+Y\partial_{YY}u_{p}^{(0)}+\partial_{Y}u_{p}^{(0)}

\displaystyle-u_{p}^{(0)}\big(\partial_{\theta}u_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,1)+v_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,1)+v_{p}^{(1)}\big)-(u^{\prime}_{e}(1)Y+u_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,1))\partial_{\theta}u_{p}^{(0)}

\displaystyle-(\partial_{r}v_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,1)+v_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,1))Y\partial_{Y}u_{p}^{(0)}-(u_{e}^{\prime}(1)+Y\partial_{Y}u_{p}^{(0)}+u_{e}(1))v_{p}^{(1)}.

2.3.
Prandtl expansions near
\partial B_{r_{0}}

Similarly as above, we introduce the scaled variable
Z=\frac{r-r_{0}}{\varepsilon}\in[0,+\infty)
and make the following Prandtl expansions near
\partial B_{r_{0}}

\displaystyle u^{\varepsilon}=u_{e}^{(0)}(\theta,r)+\widehat{u}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Z)+\varepsilon\big[u_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r)+\widehat{u}_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Z)\big]+\varepsilon^{2}\big[u_{e}^{(2)}(\theta,r)+\widehat{u}_{p}^{(2)}(\theta,Z)\big]+\text{h.o.t.},

\displaystyle v^{\varepsilon}=v_{e}^{(0)}(\theta,r)+\widehat{v}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Z)+\varepsilon\big[v_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r)+\widehat{v}_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Z)\big]+\varepsilon^{2}\big[v_{e}^{(2)}(\theta,r)+\widehat{v}_{p}^{(2)}(\theta,Z)\big]+\text{h.o.t.},

\displaystyle p^{\varepsilon}=p_{e}^{(0)}(\theta,r)+\widehat{p}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Z)+\varepsilon\big[p_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r)+\widehat{p}_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Z)\big]+\varepsilon^{2}\big[p_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r)+\widehat{p}_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Z)\big]+\text{h.o.t.},

where as
Z\rightarrow+\infty

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}^{l}\partial_{Z}^{m}\widehat{v}_{p}^{(i)}(\theta,Z)\rightarrow 0,\ \partial_{\theta}^{l}\partial_{Z}^{m}\widehat{p}_{p}^{(i)}(\theta,Z)\rightarrow 0,

here
l,m\geq 0,i=0,1,\cdots,
and there hold the following boundary conditions

\displaystyle u_{e}^{(0)}(\theta,r_{0})+\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,0)=\beta+\eta g(\theta),\ u_{e}^{(i)}(\theta,r_{0})+\hat{u}_{p}^{(i)}(\theta,0)=0,\ i\geq 1,

\displaystyle v_{e}^{(i)}(\theta,r_{0})+\hat{v}_{p}^{(i)}(\theta,0)=0,i\geq 0,\ \lim_{Z\rightarrow+\infty}(\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Z),\partial_{Z}\hat{u}_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Z))=(0,0).

2.3.1.
Equations for
(\hat{v}_{p}^{(0)},\hat{p}_{p}^{(0)})

By substituting the above expansions into (
1
) and collecting the
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
order terms, we get

\displaystyle\partial_{Z}\hat{v}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Z)=0,\ \partial_{Z}\hat{p}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Z)=0,

which together with (
2.12
) implies

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{p}^{(0)}=0,\ \hat{p}_{p}^{(0)}=0.

2.3.2.
Equations for
(u_{p}^{(0)},v_{p}^{(1)},p_{p}^{(1)})

By substituting the above expansions into (
1
) and collecting the
\varepsilon
-zeroth order terms, we obtain
the following steady Prandtl equations for
(\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)},\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)})

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(u_{e}(r_{0})+\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}\big)\partial_{\theta}\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}+\big(\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)}-\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,0)\big)r_{0}\partial_{Z}\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}-r_{0}\partial_{ZZ}\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}=0,\\[5.0pt]
\partial_{\theta}\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}+r_{0}\partial_{Z}\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)}=0,\\[5.0pt]
\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Z)=\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta+2\pi,Z),\ \hat{v}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Z)=\hat{v}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta+2\pi,Z),\\[5.0pt]
\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}\big|_{Z=0}=\beta+\eta g(\theta)-u_{e}(r_{0}),\ \lim\limits_{Z\rightarrow+\infty}\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}=\lim\limits_{Z\rightarrow+\infty}\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)}=0\end{array}\right.

and the pressure
\hat{p}_{p}^{(1)}
satisfies

\displaystyle r_{0}\partial_{Z}\hat{p}_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Z)=(\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)})^{2}(\theta,Z)+2u_{e}(r_{0})\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Z),\ \lim_{Z\rightarrow+\infty}\hat{p}_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Z)=0.

(2.30)

2.3.3.
Equations for
(\hat{u}_{p}^{(1)},\hat{v}_{p}^{(2)})

By substituting the above expansions into the first and third equation of (
1
) and collecting the
\varepsilon
-order terms, we obtain
the following linearized steady Prandtl equations for
(\hat{u}_{p}^{(1)},\hat{v}_{p}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(u_{e}(r_{0})+\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}\big)\partial_{\theta}\hat{u}_{p}^{(1)}+\big(v_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r_{0})+\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)}\big)r_{0}\partial_{Z}\hat{u}_{p}^{(1)}+\hat{u}_{p}^{(1)}\partial_{\theta}\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}\\[5.0pt]
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+(\hat{v}_{p}^{(2)}-\hat{v}_{p}^{(2)}(\theta,0))r_{0}\partial_{Z}\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}-r_{0}\partial_{ZZ}\hat{u}_{p}^{(1)}=\hat{f}_{1}(\theta,Z),\\[5.0pt]
\partial_{\theta}\hat{u}_{p}^{(1)}+r_{0}\partial_{Z}\hat{v}_{p}^{(2)}+\partial_{Z}(Z\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)})=0,\\[5.0pt]
\hat{u}_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Z)=\hat{u}_{p}^{(1)}(\theta+2\pi,Z),\ \hat{v}_{p}^{(2)}(\theta,Z)=\hat{v}_{p}^{(2)}(\theta+2\pi,Z),\\[5.0pt]
\hat{u}_{p}^{(1)}\big|_{Z=0}=-u_{e}^{(1)}\big|_{r=r_{0}},\ \lim\limits_{Z\rightarrow+\infty}\partial_{Z}\hat{u}_{p}^{(1)}=\lim\limits_{Z\rightarrow+\infty}\hat{v}_{p}^{(2)}=0,\end{array}\right.

where

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{1}(\theta,Y)=

\displaystyle-\partial_{\theta}\hat{p}_{p}^{(1)}+Z\partial_{ZZ}\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}+\partial_{Z}\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}

\displaystyle-\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}(\partial_{\theta}u_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r_{0})+v_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r_{0})+\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)})-(u^{\prime}_{e}(r_{0})Z+u_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r_{0}))\partial_{\theta}\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}

\displaystyle-(r_{0}\partial_{r}v_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r_{0})+v_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r_{0})+\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)})Z\partial_{Z}u_{p}^{(0)}-[u_{e}(r_{0})+r_{0}u^{\prime}_{e}(r_{0})]\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)}.

Before performing the higher order expansions we then aim to solve the above Euler equations and Prandtl equations.

2.4.
Solvabilities of Euler equations and Prandtl equations

The order in which we solve the equation is as follows

\displaystyle(u_{e}(r),0)\rightarrow(u_{p}^{(0)},v_{p}^{(1)})/(\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)},\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)})\rightarrow(u_{e}^{(1)},v_{e}^{(1)})\rightarrow(u_{p}^{(1)},v_{p}^{(2)})/(\hat{u}_{p}^{(1)},\hat{v}_{p}^{(2)}).

2.4.1.
Prandtl system and its solvability

We first derive some necessary conditions for the solvability of Prandtl equations, one can also refer to Lemma 2.1 in
[
6
]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

(Batchelor-Wood formula
[
22
]
[
23
]
) Let
\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(\theta)d\theta=\int_{0}^{2\pi}g(\theta)d\theta=0
. If the nonlinear Prandtle equations (
2.2.2
) has a solution
(u_{p}^{(0)},v_{p}^{(1)})
and (
2.3.2
) have a solution
(\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)},\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)})
which satisfy

\displaystyle u_{e}(1)+u_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Y)>0,\ \forall Y\leq 0;\quad u_{e}(r_{0})+\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Z)>0,\ \forall Z\geq 0,

\displaystyle\|u_{p}^{(0)}\|_{\infty}+\|\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)}\|_{\infty}\leq M,

where
M>0
is a constant, then there hold

\displaystyle u_{e}^{2}(1)=\alpha^{2}+\frac{\eta^{2}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f^{2}(\theta)d\theta,

(2.37)

and

\displaystyle u_{e}^{2}(r_{0})=\beta^{2}+\frac{\eta^{2}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}g^{2}(\theta)d\theta.

(2.38)

Proof.

We only need to prove
(\ref{BW formula})
since
(\ref{BW formula-1})
can be derived similarly.
We introduce the von Mises variable

\displaystyle\psi=\int_{0}^{Y}\big(u_{e}(1)+u_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,z)\big)dz,\ \mathcal{U}^{(0)}(\theta,\psi)=u_{e}(1)+u_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,Y).

Then from (
2.2.2
), we deduce that
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}2\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{\theta}=\big((\mathcal{U}^{(0)})^{2}\big)_{\psi\psi},\\[5.0pt]
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}(\theta,\psi)=\mathcal{U}^{(0)}(\theta+2\pi,\psi),\\[5.0pt]
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}\big|_{\psi=0}=\alpha+\eta f(\theta),\ \ \lim_{\psi\rightarrow-\infty}\mathcal{U}^{(0)}=u_{e}(1).\end{array}\right.

Here we have used the facts:

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}u_{p}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{\theta}+\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{\psi}\int_{0}^{Y}\partial_{\theta}u_{p}^{(0)}(\theta,z)\big)dz

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{\theta}+\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{\psi}\big(v_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,0)-v_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Y)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{\theta}-\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{\psi}\big(v^{(1)}(\theta,1)+v_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Y)\big),

\displaystyle\partial_{Y}u_{p}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}^{(0)}_{\psi}\mathcal{U}^{(0)}.

Integrating the first equation in (
2.4.1
) from
0
to
2\pi
about
\theta
leads to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\psi^{2}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}(\mathcal{U}^{(0)})^{2}(\theta,\psi)d\theta=0.

Notice that
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
is bounded at
\psi\rightarrow-\infty
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\psi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}(\mathcal{U}^{(0)})^{2}(\theta,\psi)d\theta=0.

Therefore combining the boundary condition in (
2.4.1
), we deduce that

\displaystyle u_{e}^{2}(1)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(\alpha+\eta f(\theta)\big)^{2}d\theta

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}+\frac{\alpha\eta}{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(\theta)d\theta+\frac{\eta^{2}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f^{2}(\theta)d\theta

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}+\frac{\eta^{2}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f^{2}(\theta)d\theta.

Thus, we complete the proof of this lemma.

MM

\square

Next we aim to solve the steady Prandtl equations (
2.2.2
), one can refer to Corollary 2.2 in
[
6
]
.

Proposition 2.2
.

There exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
and any
j,k,l\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, the equations (
2.4.1
) have a unique solution
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}\big(\mathcal{U}^{(0)}-u_{e}(1)\big)\Big|^{2}\big<\psi\big>^{2l}d\theta d\psi\leq C(j,k,l)\eta^{2},

here
\big<\psi\big>=\sqrt{1+\psi^{2}}
.

Proof.

We will use the contraction mapping theorem to prove the desired conclusions and divide the proof into five steps.

Step 1:

Derivation of equivalent equations.

Let
Q(\theta,\psi):=(\mathcal{U}^{(0)})^{2}(\theta,\psi)-u_{e}^{2}(1)
and we rewrite (
2.4.1
) as

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}Q_{\theta}=\mathcal{U}^{(0)}Q_{\psi\psi},\\[7.0pt]
Q(\theta,\psi)=Q(\theta+2\pi,\psi),\\[5.0pt]
Q\big|_{\psi=0}=\alpha^{2}+2\alpha\eta f(\theta)+\eta^{2}f^{2}(\theta)-u_{e}^{2}(1),\ Q\big|_{\psi\rightarrow-\infty}=0.\end{array}\right.

Defining

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(Q)=Q-2u_{e}(1)\sqrt{Q+u_{e}^{2}(1)}+2u_{e}^{2}(1),

then (
2.4.1
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}Q_{\theta}-u_{e}(1)Q_{\psi\psi}=(\mathcal{G}(Q))_{\theta},\\[7.0pt]
Q(\theta,\psi)=Q(\theta+2\pi,\psi),\\[5.0pt]
Q\big|_{\psi=0}=\alpha^{2}+2\alpha\eta f(\theta)+\eta^{2}f^{2}(\theta)-u_{e}^{2}(1),\ Q\big|_{\psi\rightarrow-\infty}=0.\end{array}\right.

Let
Q_{0}
be the solution to

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(Q_{0})_{\theta}=u_{e}(1)(Q_{0})_{\psi\psi},\\[7.0pt]
Q_{0}(\theta,\psi)=Q_{0}(\theta+2\pi,\psi),\\[5.0pt]
Q_{0}\big|_{\psi=0}=\alpha^{2}+2\alpha\eta f(\theta)+\eta^{2}f^{2}(\theta)-u_{e}^{2}(1),\ Q_{0}\big|_{\psi\rightarrow-\infty}=0,\end{array}\right.

which will be solved in Appendix A, then (
2.4.1
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}Q_{\theta}-u_{e}(1)Q_{\psi\psi}=(\mathcal{H}(Q))_{\theta},\\[7.0pt]
Q(\theta,\psi)=Q(\theta+2\pi,\psi),\\[5.0pt]
Q\big|_{\psi=0}=0,\ Q\big|_{\psi\rightarrow-\infty}=0,\end{array}\right.

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(Q)=Q+Q_{0}-2u_{e}(1)\sqrt{Q+Q_{0}+u_{e}^{2}(1)}+2u_{e}^{2}(1).

Defining the linear operator
\mathcal{L}:\Lambda\longmapsto\Phi
such that

\displaystyle\Phi=\mathcal{L}\Lambda\Longleftrightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Phi_{\theta}-u_{e}(1)\Phi_{\psi\psi}=\Lambda_{\theta},\\[7.0pt]
\Phi(\theta,\psi)=\Phi(\theta+2\pi,\psi),\\[5.0pt]
\Phi\big|_{\psi=0}=0,\ \Phi\big|_{\psi\rightarrow-\infty}=0,\end{array}\right.

then (
2.4.1
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}Q=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}(Q)),\\[7.0pt]
Q(\theta,\psi)=Q(\theta+2\pi,\psi),\\[5.0pt]
Q\big|_{\psi=0}=0,\ Q\big|_{\psi=-\infty}=0.\end{array}\right.

Defining the function space
X
as follows

\displaystyle X=\bigg\{

\displaystyle Q:Q(\theta,\psi)=Q(\theta+2\pi,\psi),\ Q\big|_{\psi=0}=Q\big|_{\psi\rightarrow-\infty}=0,

\displaystyle\quad\quad\|Q\|_{X}^{2}:=\sum\limits_{j+k\leq m,l\geq 0}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}Q\big|^{2}\big<\psi\big>^{l}d\theta d\psi<+\infty\bigg\}

and a ball
B_{0}
in
X

\displaystyle B_{0}=\big\{Q\in X:\|Q\|_{X}\leq\nu\big\},

here
m
is a positive integer and
\nu
is a small number which will be determined later.

In the next three steps we aim to verify that
\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H}
is a contraction map from
B_{0}
to
B_{0}
with suitable small
\nu
.

Step 2:

Boundedness of
\mathcal{L}
in
X
. In this step, we prove that for any
m\geq 0,l\geq 0
, there holds

\displaystyle\sum_{j+k\leq m}\big\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\partial^{k}_{\psi}\Phi\big<\psi\big>^{l}\big\|_{2}\leq C(m,l)\sum_{j+k\leq m,q\leq l}\big\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\partial^{k}_{\psi}\Lambda\big<\psi\big>^{q}\big\|_{2}.

(2.67)

First, we prove (
2.67
) for
l=0
. Multiplying the equation in (
2.4.1
) by
\Phi
and integrating with respect to
(\theta,\psi)\in(0,2\pi)\times(-\infty,0)
, we obtain for any
\lambda>0

\displaystyle u_{e}(1)\|\Phi_{\psi}\|_{2}\leq C(\lambda)\|\Lambda\|_{2}+\lambda\|\Phi_{\theta}\|_{2}.

(2.68)

Then, multiplying the equation in (
2.4.1
) by
\Phi_{\theta}
and integrating with respect to
(\theta,\psi)\in(0,2\pi)\times(-\infty,0)
, one has

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{\theta}\|_{2}\leq C(\lambda)\|\Lambda_{\theta}\|_{2}+\lambda\|\Phi_{\theta}\|_{2}.

(2.69)

Combining (
2.68
)-(
2.69
) and choosing small
\lambda>0
we get

\displaystyle\|(\Phi_{\psi},\Phi_{\theta})\|_{2}\leq C\|(\Lambda,\Lambda_{\theta})\|_{2}.

(2.70)

Integrating (
2.4.1
) with respect to
\theta\in(0,2\pi)
gives

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{d\psi^{2}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Phi(\theta,\psi)d\theta=0,

which and
\Phi|_{\psi=0}=\Phi|_{\psi\rightarrow-\infty}=0
imply

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Phi(\theta,\psi)d\theta=0.

(2.71)

Due to (
2.71
) and the Poincaré inequality we have

\displaystyle\|\Phi\|_{2}\leq C\|\Phi_{\theta}\|_{2}\leq C\|(\Lambda,\Lambda_{\theta})\|_{2}.

(2.72)

For any
j\geq 0,k\geq 2
, from the equation (
2.4.1
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}\Phi\|_{2}\leq C(\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k-2}\Phi_{\theta}\|_{2}+\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k-2}\Lambda_{\theta}\|_{2}).

(2.73)

For any
j\geq 0
, applying
\partial^{j}_{\theta}
to the equation in (
2.4.1
), multiplying the resultant equation by
\partial^{j}_{\theta}\Phi
, integrating with respect to
(\theta,\psi)\in(0,2\pi)\times(-\infty,0)
and using the Young inequality one has

\displaystyle\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\partial_{\psi}\Phi\|_{2}\leq C\|\partial^{j+1}_{\theta}\Lambda\|_{2}+C\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\Phi\|_{2}.

(2.74)

For any
j\geq 0
, multiplying the equation in (
2.4.1
) by
\partial^{2j-1}_{\theta}\Phi
, integrating with respect to
(\theta,\psi)\in(0,2\pi)\times(-\infty,0)
, one can get

\displaystyle\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\Phi\|^{2}_{2}=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\partial^{2j-1}_{\theta}\Phi\Lambda_{\theta}d\theta d\psi\leq C\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\Phi\|_{2}\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\Lambda\|_{2}.

(2.75)

Based on (
2.73
), (
2.74
) and (
2.75
), we obtain that for any
m\geq 2
, there holds

\displaystyle\sum_{j+k=m}\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\partial^{k}_{\psi}\Phi\|_{2}\leq C\sum_{j+k\leq m-1}\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\partial^{k}_{\psi}\Phi\|_{2}+C\sum_{j+k\leq m}\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\partial^{k}_{\psi}\Lambda\|_{2}.

Thus, by induction and (
2.70
), (
2.72
), we deduce that for any
m\geq 0
, there holds

\displaystyle\sum_{j+k\leq m}\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\partial^{k}_{\psi}\Phi\|_{2}\leq C(m)\sum_{j+k\leq m}\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\partial^{k}_{\psi}\Lambda\|_{2}.

(2.76)

Next, we prove (
2.67
) for
m\leq 1,l\geq 1
.
Multiplying the equation in (
2.4.1
) by
\Phi\psi^{2l}
, integrating with respect to
(\theta,\psi)\in(0,2\pi)\times(-\infty,0)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Phi_{\psi}^{2}\psi^{2l}d\theta d\psi

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Phi^{2}\psi^{2(l-1)}d\theta d\psi

\displaystyle\quad+C(\lambda)\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Lambda^{2}\psi^{2l}d\theta d\psi+\lambda\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Phi_{\theta}^{2}\psi^{2l}d\theta d\psi.

(2.77)

Multiplying the equation in (
2.4.1
) by
\Phi_{\theta}\psi^{2l}
, integrating with respect to
(\theta,\psi)\in(0,2\pi)\times(-\infty,0)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Phi_{\theta}^{2}\psi^{2l}d\theta d\psi

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Phi_{\psi}^{2}\psi^{2(l-1)}d\theta d\psi+C\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Lambda_{\theta}^{2}\psi^{2l}d\theta d\psi.

(2.78)

Combining (
2.77
)-(
2.78
), using the Poincaré inequality and choosing small
\lambda>0
we get

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(\Phi^{2}+\Phi_{\psi}^{2}+\Phi_{\theta}^{2}\big)\psi^{2l}d\theta d\psi

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(\Phi^{2}+\Phi_{\psi}^{2}+\Phi_{\theta}^{2}\big)\psi^{2(l-1)}d\theta d\psi+C\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(\Lambda^{2}\psi^{2l}+\Lambda_{\theta}^{2}\psi^{2l}\big)d\theta d\psi.

By induction on
l
and using (
2.76
), we deduce that for any
l\geq 1
, there holds

\displaystyle\big\|\big(\Phi_{\psi},\Phi_{\theta},\Phi\big)\psi^{l}\big\|_{2}\leq C(l)\sum_{q\leq l}\big\|\big(\Lambda,\Lambda_{\theta}\big)\psi^{q}\big\|_{2}.

(2.79)

Finally, we prove (
2.67
) any
m\geq 2,l\geq 1
. When
j+k=m,k\geq 2
, from the equation (
2.4.1
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}\Phi\psi^{l}\Big\|_{2}\leq C\Big(\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k-2}\partial_{\theta}\Phi\psi^{l}\Big\|_{2}+\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k-2}\partial_{\theta}\Lambda\psi^{l}\Big\|_{2}\Big).

(2.80)

Applying
\partial^{m-1}_{\theta}
to the equation in (
2.4.1
), multiplying the resultant equation by
\partial^{m-1}_{\theta}\Phi\psi^{2l}
, integrating with respect to
(\theta,\psi)\in(0,2\pi)\times(-\infty,0)
and using the Young inequality one has

\displaystyle\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{m-1}\partial_{\psi}\Phi\psi^{l}\Big\|_{2}\leq C\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{m}\Lambda\psi^{l}\Big\|_{2}+C\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{m-1}\Phi\psi^{l}\Big\|_{2}+C(l)\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{m-1}\Phi\psi^{l-1}\Big\|_{2}.

(2.81)

Multiplying the equation in (
2.4.1
) by
\partial^{2m-1}_{\theta}\Phi\psi^{2l}
, integrating with respect to
(\theta,\psi)\in(0,2\pi)\times(-\infty,0)
, one can get

\displaystyle\Big\|\partial^{m}_{\theta}\Phi\psi^{l}\Big\|_{2}\leq C\Big\|\partial^{m}_{\theta}\Lambda\psi^{l}\Big\|_{2}+C(l)\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{m-1}\partial_{\psi}\Phi\psi^{l-1}\Big\|_{2}.

(2.82)

Thanks to (
2.80
), (
2.81
) and (
2.82
), we obtain that for any
m\geq 2,l\geq 1
, there holds

\displaystyle\sum_{j+k=m}\Big\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\partial^{k}_{\psi}\Phi\psi^{l}\Big\|_{2}\leq C(m,l)\sum_{j+k\leq m-1,q\leq l}\Big\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\partial^{k}_{\psi}\Phi\psi^{q}\Big\|_{2}+C(m,l)\sum_{j+k\leq m}\Big\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\partial^{k}_{\psi}\Lambda\psi^{l}\Big\|_{2}.

Thus, by induction
m
and using (
2.79
), we deduce that for any
m\geq 2,l\geq 1
, there holds

\displaystyle\sum_{j+k\leq m}\Big\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\partial^{k}_{\psi}\Phi\psi^{l}\Big\|_{2}\leq C(m,l)\sum_{j+k\leq m,q\leq l}\Big\|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\partial^{k}_{\psi}\Lambda\psi^{q}\Big\|_{2}.

(2.83)

This complete the proof of (
2.67
).
Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}\Lambda\|_{X}\leq C\|\Lambda\|_{X},\ \forall\Lambda\in X.

(2.84)

Step 3:

\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H}
is a continuous map from
B_{0}
to
B_{0}
.
In this section, we first prove that for any
m\geq 2,l\geq 0
, there holds

\displaystyle\sum_{j+k\leq m}\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}\mathcal{H}(Q)\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{2}\leq C(m,l)\Big(\sum_{j+k\leq m}\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}Q\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{2}+\sum_{j+k\leq m}\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}Q_{0}\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{2}\Big)^{2}.

(2.85)

Set

\displaystyle\tilde{H}(x)=x-2u_{e}(1)\sqrt{x+u_{e}^{2}(1)}+2u^{2}_{e}(1),\ |x|\ll u_{e}^{2}(1),

then
\mathcal{H}(Q)=\tilde{H}(Q+Q_{0}).
Direct computation gives

\displaystyle|\tilde{H}^{\prime}(x)|\leq C|x|,\ |\tilde{H}^{(k)}(x)|\leq C,\ k\geq 2.

(2.86)

Since

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(Q)=\big(\sqrt{Q+Q_{0}+u_{e}^{2}(1)}-u_{e}(1)\big)^{2}=\bigg(\frac{Q+Q_{0}}{\sqrt{Q+Q_{0}+u_{e}^{2}(1)}+u_{e}(1)}\bigg)^{2},

it’s easy to get

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}(Q)\big<\psi\big>^{l}\leq C\|Q+Q_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\big(Q^{2}\big<\psi\big>^{l}+Q_{0}^{2}\big<\psi\big>^{l}\big),\ l\geq 0.

Using (
2.86
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{\psi}\mathcal{H}(Q)\big)^{2}\big<\psi\big>^{l}\leq

\displaystyle C\|Q+Q_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\big(Q_{\psi}^{2}\big<\psi\big>^{l}+(Q_{0})_{\psi}^{2}\big<\psi\big>^{l}\big),\ l\geq 0,

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{H}(Q)\big)^{2}\big<\psi\big>^{l}\leq

\displaystyle C\|Q+Q_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\big(Q_{\theta}^{2}\big<\psi\big>^{l}+(Q_{0})_{\theta}^{2}\big<\psi\big>^{l}\big),\ l\geq 0.

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{j+k\leq 1}\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}\mathcal{H}(Q)\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|Q+Q_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\Big(\sum_{j+k\leq 1}\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}Q\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{2}+\sum_{j+k\leq 1}\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}Q_{0}\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{2}\Big).

(2.87)

Since

\displaystyle\partial_{\psi\theta}\mathcal{H}(Q)=\tilde{H}^{\prime}(Q+Q_{0})(\partial_{\psi\theta}Q+\partial_{\psi\theta}Q_{0})+\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}(Q+Q_{0})(\partial_{\theta}Q+\partial_{\theta}Q_{0})(\partial_{\psi}Q+\partial_{\psi}Q_{0}),

thus, using (
2.86
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\|\partial_{\psi\theta}\mathcal{H}(Q)\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|Q+Q_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\Big(\Big\|Q_{\psi\theta}\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{L^{2}}+\Big\|(Q_{0})_{\psi\theta}\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{L^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+C\big\|Q_{\psi}+(Q_{0})_{\psi}\big\|_{L^{4}}\big\|(Q_{\theta}+(Q_{0})_{\theta})\big<\psi\big>^{l}\big\|_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|Q+Q_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\Big(\Big\|Q_{\psi\theta}\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{L^{2}}+\Big\|(Q_{0})_{\psi\theta}\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{L^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+C\big\|Q_{\psi}+(Q_{0})_{\psi}\big\|_{H^{1}}\big\|(Q_{\theta}+(Q_{0})_{\theta})\big<\psi\big>^{l}\big\|_{H^{1}}.

Hence by Sobolev imbedding, we deduce that

\displaystyle\Big\|\partial_{\psi\theta}\mathcal{H}(Q)\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{2}\leq C\Big(\sum_{j+k\leq 2}\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}Q\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{2}+\sum_{j+k\leq 2}\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}Q_{0}\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{2}\Big)^{2}.

Same estimates hold for
\Big\|\partial_{\theta\theta}\mathcal{H}(Q)\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{2}
and
\Big\|\partial_{\psi\psi}\mathcal{H}(Q)\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{2}
. Thus, combing the estimate (
2.4.1
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\sum_{j+k\leq 2}\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}\mathcal{H}(Q)\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{2}\leq C\Big(\sum_{j+k\leq 2}\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}Q\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{2}+\sum_{j+k\leq 2}\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}Q_{0}\big<\psi\big>^{l}\Big\|_{2}\Big)^{2}.

Using (
2.86
) and repeating the above arguments, we obtain (
2.85
).

Consequently, if we take
\nu=\|Q_{0}\|_{X}
and
\eta
small enough, then

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}\mathcal{H}(Q)\|_{X}\leq C\|\mathcal{H}(Q)\|_{X}\leq 4C\nu^{2}\leq 4C\eta\nu\leq\nu,\ \forall Q\in B_{0},

here we have used (
5.6
). Thus,
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{H}
is a continuous map from
B_{0}
to
B_{0}
for small
\eta
.

Step 4:

\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H}
is a contraction map in
B_{0}
.

Noting firstly that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(Q_{1})-\mathcal{H}(Q_{2})

\displaystyle=(Q_{1}-Q_{2})\bigg(1-\frac{2u_{e}(1)}{\sqrt{Q_{1}+Q_{0}+u_{e}^{2}(1)}+\sqrt{Q_{2}+Q_{0}+u_{e}^{2}(1)}}\bigg)

and

\displaystyle 1-\frac{2u_{e}(1)}{\sqrt{Q_{1}+Q_{0}+u_{e}^{2}(1)}+\sqrt{Q_{2}+Q_{0}+u_{e}^{2}(1)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{Q_{1}+Q_{0}}{\big(\sqrt{Q_{1}+Q_{0}+u_{e}^{2}(1)}+\sqrt{Q_{2}+Q_{0}+u_{e}^{2}(1)}\big)\big(\sqrt{Q_{1}+Q_{0}+u_{e}^{2}(1)}+u_{e}(1)\big)}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{Q_{2}+Q_{0}}{\big(\sqrt{Q_{1}+Q_{0}+u_{e}^{2}(1)}+\sqrt{Q_{2}+Q_{0}+u_{e}^{2}(1)}\big)\big(\sqrt{Q_{2}+Q_{0}+u_{e}^{2}(1)}+u_{e}(1)\big)}.

With the help of the similar arguments in Step 3, we can obtain that there exist
\eta_{0}>0
such that for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
, there holds

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{L}\mathcal{H}(Q_{1})-\mathcal{L}\mathcal{H}(Q_{2})\big\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|\mathcal{H}(Q_{1})-\mathcal{H}(Q_{2})\big\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|Q_{1}-Q_{2}\big\|_{X}\big(\|Q_{0}\|_{X}+\|Q_{1}\|_{X}+\|Q_{2}\|_{X}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\|Q_{0}\|_{X}\big\|Q_{1}-Q_{2}\big\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\big\|Q_{1}-Q_{2}\big\|_{X},

that is,
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{H}
is a contraction map in
B_{0}
.

Step 5:

Existence and uniqueness of the system (
2.4.1
).
By the standard contraction mapping principle, we know that there exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
and any
j,k,l\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, the equation (
2.4.1
) has a unique solution
\mathcal{U}^{(0)}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{\psi}^{k}\big(\mathcal{U}^{(0)}-u_{e}(1)\big)\Big|^{2}\big<\psi\big>^{2l}d\theta d\psi\leq C(j,k,l)\eta^{2},

this completes the proof of this proposition.

MM

\square

Return to the equations (
2.2.2
), we have the following result.

Proposition 2.3
.

There exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
, the equations (
2.2.2
) have a unique solution
(u_{p}^{(0)},v_{p}^{(1)})
which satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sum_{j+k\leq m}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{Y}^{k}(u_{p}^{(0)},v_{p}^{(1)})\Big|^{2}\big<Y\big>^{2l}d\theta dY\leq C(m,l)\eta^{2},\ \forall m,l\geq 0,\\
&\int_{0}^{2\pi}v_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Y)d\theta=0,\ \forall Y\leq 0.\end{aligned}

(2.88)

Finally, solving (
2.18
), we obtain
p_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Y)
which decay very fast as
Y\rightarrow-\infty
.

By the same argument, we can obtain the well-posedness of equations (
2.3.2
).

Proposition 2.4
.

There exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
, the equations (
2.3.2
) have a unique solution
(\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)},\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)})
which satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sum_{j+k\leq m}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{Y}^{k}(\hat{u}_{p}^{(0)},\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)})\Big|^{2}\big<Y\big>^{2l}d\theta dY\leq C(m,l)\eta^{2},\ m,l\geq 0,\\
&\int_{0}^{2\pi}\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Z)d\theta=0,\ \forall Z\geq 0.\end{aligned}

(2.89)

Similarly, we can obtain
\hat{p}_{p}^{(1)}
by solving (
2.30
) and
\hat{p}_{p}^{(1)}
decay very fast as
Z\rightarrow+\infty
.

Here we give a general strategy for constructing high order approximate solution. We first construct
(u_{e}^{(1)},v_{e}^{(1)},p_{e}^{(1)})
by solving the linearized Euler equations (
2.1.2
) and
(u_{p}^{(1)},v_{p}^{(2)})
by solving the linearized Prandtl equations (
2.2.3
). Then, because
A_{1\infty}:=\lim_{Y\rightarrow-\infty}u_{p}^{(1)}
is a nonzero constant, we need to change it to
\tilde{u}_{p}^{(1)}=u_{p}^{(1)}-A_{1\infty}
. Finally, we modify
u_{e}^{(1)}
into
\tilde{u}_{e}^{(1)}
by adding a radial function, see (
2.136
) for the details. Notice the structure of the linearized Euler equations (
2.1.2
) and the fact that only the value of
u_{e}^{(1)}
at
r=1
appear in the equation (
2.2.3
), we can easily deduce that the modified
(\tilde{u}_{e}^{(1)},\tilde{v}_{e}^{(1)},\tilde{p}_{e}^{(1)})
and
(\tilde{u}_{p}^{(1)},\tilde{v}_{p}^{(2)})
still satisfies the equation (
2.1.2
) and (
2.2.3
). The higher order approximate solutions
(\tilde{u}_{e}^{(i)},\tilde{v}_{e}^{(i)},\tilde{p}_{e}^{(i)})(i\geq 2)
and
(\tilde{u}_{p}^{(i)},\tilde{v}_{p}^{(i+1)})(i\geq 2)
can be constructed by the same approach.

2.4.2.
Linearized Euler equations for
(u_{e}^{(1)},v_{e}^{(1)},p_{e}^{(1)})
and their solvability

Proposition 2.5
.

There exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
, the linearized Euler equations (
2.1.2
) have a solution
(u_{e}^{(1)},v_{e}^{(1)},p_{e}^{(1)})
which satisfies

\displaystyle\|\partial^{k}_{\theta}\partial^{j}_{r}(u_{e}^{(1)},v_{e}^{(1)})\|_{2}\leq C(k,j)\eta,\ \forall j,k\geq 0.

(2.90)

Proof.

Eliminating pressure
p_{e}^{(1)}
in the equation (
2.1.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle-u_{e}(r)\Big(\partial_{rr}v_{e}^{(1)}+\frac{\partial_{\theta\theta}v_{e}^{(1)}}{r^{2}}+\frac{3\partial_{r}v_{e}^{(1)}}{r}\Big)+v_{e}^{(1)}\Big(u^{\prime\prime}_{e}(r)+\frac{u^{\prime}_{e}(r)}{r}-\frac{2u_{e}(r)}{r^{2}}\Big)=0.

Notice that
\triangle=\partial_{rr}+\frac{\partial_{\theta\theta}}{r^{2}}+\frac{\partial_{r}}{r}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle-u_{e}(r)\triangle(rv_{e}^{(1)})+\Big(u^{\prime\prime}_{e}(r)+\frac{u^{\prime}_{e}(r)}{r}-\frac{u_{e}(r)}{r^{2}}\Big)(rv_{e}^{(1)})=0,

hence we obtain the following equations for
rv_{e}^{(1)}
in
\Omega

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\triangle(rv_{e}^{(1)})+\frac{1}{u_{e}(r)}\Big(u^{\prime\prime}_{e}(r)+\frac{u^{\prime}_{e}(r)}{r}-\frac{u_{e}(r)}{r^{2}}\Big)(rv_{e}^{(1)})=0,\\[5.0pt]
rv_{e}^{(1)}|_{r=1}=-v_{p}^{(1)}|_{Y=0},\quad rv_{e}^{(1)}|_{r=r_{0}}=-r_{0}\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)}|_{Z=0}.\end{array}\right.

Firstly, we deduce that equations (
2.4.2
) a unique solution
rv_{e}^{(1)}
.
In fact, we assume that
f(\theta,r)
solving the equations

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\triangle f+\frac{1}{u_{e}(r)}\Big(u^{\prime\prime}_{e}(r)+\frac{u^{\prime}_{e}(r)}{r}-\frac{u_{e}(r)}{r^{2}}\Big)f=0,\\[5.0pt]
f|_{r=1}=0,\ f|_{r=r_{0}}=0,\end{array}\right.

then multiple
rf
and integrating in
\Omega
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\Big(r(\partial_{r}f)^{2}+(\partial_{\theta}f)^{2}+\frac{1}{u_{e}(r)}\Big(ru^{\prime\prime}_{e}(r)+u^{\prime}_{e}(r)-\frac{u_{e}(r)}{r}\Big)f^{2}\Big)d\theta dr=0.

Let
g=\frac{f}{u_{e}}
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\Big(ru^{2}_{e}(r)(\partial_{r}g)^{2}+\frac{u_{e}^{2}(r)g^{2}_{\theta}}{r}-\frac{u_{e}^{2}(r)g^{2}}{r}\Big)d\theta dr=0.

Notice that
\int_{0}^{2\pi}g(\theta,r)d\theta=0,\ \forall r\in[r_{0},1]
, by Poincaré inequality, we deduce that
g=0
, hence
f=0
. Thus, equation (
2.4.2
) has a unique solution.

Then, notice (
2.88
) and (
2.89
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\|\partial^{k}_{\theta}\partial^{j}_{r}v_{e}^{(1)}\|_{2}\leq C(k,j)\eta,\ \forall k,j\geq 0.

Moreover, due to
\int_{0}^{2\pi}v_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,0)d\theta=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\hat{v}_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,0)d\theta=0
and the divergence-free condition, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}v_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r)d\theta=0,\ \forall r\in[r_{0},1].

(2.96)

Then we can construct
u_{e}^{(1)}
by solving the following equation

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{\theta}u_{e}^{(1)}+\partial_{r}(rv_{e}^{(1)})=0,\\[5.0pt]
u_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r)=u_{e}^{(1)}(\theta+2\pi,r).\end{array}\right.

After obtaining
(u_{e}^{(1)},v_{e}^{(1)})
, we construct
p_{e}^{(1)}
as following

\displaystyle p_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,r):=\phi(r)-\int_{0}^{\theta}[u_{e}(r)\partial_{\theta^{\prime}}u_{e}^{(1)}+ru^{\prime}_{e}v_{e}^{(1)}+u_{e}v_{e}^{(1)}](\theta^{\prime},r)d\theta^{\prime},

where
\phi(r)
is a function which satisfies

\displaystyle r\partial_{r}\phi(r)+u_{e}(r)\partial_{\theta}v_{e}^{(1)}(0,r)-2u_{e}(r)u_{e}^{(1)}(0,r)=0.

Combining the equations of
(u_{e}^{(1)},v_{e}^{(1)})
, it’s direct to obtain

\displaystyle u_{e}\partial_{\theta}v_{e}^{(1)}-2u_{e}u_{e}^{(1)}+r\partial_{r}p_{e}^{(1)}=0.

Hence,
(u_{e}^{(1)},v_{e}^{(1)},p_{e}^{(1)})
solves the equation (
2.1.2
) and satisfies (
2.90
).

MM

\square

2.4.3.
Linearized Prandtl equations for
(u_{p}^{(1)},v_{p}^{(2)})
,
(\hat{u}_{p}^{(1)},\hat{v}_{p}^{(2)})
and their solvabilities

In this subsubsection, we consider the solvabilities of (
2.2.3
) and (
2.3.3
). One can also refer to Proposition 2.5 in
[
6
]
.

Proposition 2.6
.

There exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
, the equations (
2.2.3
) have a unique solution
(u_{p}^{(1)},v_{p}^{(2)})
which satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sum_{j+k\leq m}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big|\partial_{\theta}^{j}\partial_{Y}^{k}\big(u_{p}^{(1)}-A_{1\infty},v_{p}^{(2)}\big)\big|^{2}\big<Y\big>^{2l}d\theta dY\leq C(m,l)\eta^{2},\ \forall m,l\geq 0,\\
&\int_{0}^{2\pi}v_{p}^{(2)}(\theta,Y)d\theta=0,\ \forall Y\leq 0,\end{aligned}

(2.99)

where

A_{1\infty}:=\lim\limits_{Y\rightarrow-\infty}u_{p}^{(1)}(\theta,Y)
is a constant which satisfies
|A_{1\infty}|\leq C\eta.

Proof.

Let
\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}((-\infty,0])
satisfy

\displaystyle\eta(0)=1,\ \int_{0}^{+\infty}\eta(y)dy=0.

For simplicity, we set

\displaystyle\bar{u}:

\displaystyle=u_{e}(1)+u_{p}^{(0)},\ \bar{v}:=v_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,1)+v_{p}^{(1)},

\displaystyle u:

\displaystyle=u_{p}^{(1)}+u_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,1)\eta(Y),\ v:=v_{p}^{(2)}-v_{p}^{(2)}(\theta,0)+Yv_{p}^{(1)}-\partial_{\theta}u_{e}^{(1)}(\theta,1)\int_{0}^{Y}\eta(z)dz.

Then, the equations (
2.2.3
) reduce to

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\bar{u}\partial_{\theta}u+\bar{v}\partial_{Y}u+u\partial_{\theta}\bar{u}+v\partial_{Y}\bar{u}-\partial_{YY}u=\tilde{f},\\[7.0pt]
\partial_{\theta}u+\partial_{Y}v=0,\\[5.0pt]
u(\theta,Y)=u(\theta+2\pi,Y),\ v(\theta,Y)=v(\theta+2\pi,Y)\\[5.0pt]
u|_{Y=0}=v|_{Y=0}=0,\ \lim\limits_{Y\rightarrow-\infty}\partial_{Y}u=0,\end{array}\right.

where
\tilde{f}(\theta,Y)
is
2\pi
-periodic function and decays fast as
Y\rightarrow-\infty
.

We can solve the equations (
2.4.3
) by considering the following approximate system.
Let
\gamma>0
be a constant, we consider the following elliptic equation

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\bar{u}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}+\bar{v}\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}+\big[\int_{Y}^{0}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}(\theta,z)dz\big]\partial_{Y}\bar{u}+u^{\gamma}\partial_{\theta}\bar{u}-\partial_{YY}u^{\gamma}-\gamma\partial_{\theta\theta}u^{\gamma}=\tilde{f},\\[7.0pt]
u^{\gamma}(\theta,Y)=u^{\gamma}(\theta+2\pi,Y),\\[5.0pt]
u^{\gamma}|_{Y=0}=0.\end{array}\right.

We expect the solution of this equation is in
\dot{H}^{1}_{0}=\{u|\partial_{\theta}u\in L^{2},\partial_{Y}u\in L^{2},u|_{Y=0}=0\}
rather than
H^{1}_{0}=\{u|u\in L^{2},\partial_{\theta}u\in L^{2},\partial_{Y}u\in L^{2},u|_{Y=0}=0\}
. Now we establish apriori estimate of equation (
2.4.3
).
Multiplying the first equation in (
2.4.3
) by
u^{\gamma}
and integrating in
(\theta,r)\in(0,2\pi)\times(-\infty,0)
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big[\bar{u}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}+\bar{v}\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}+\Big(\int_{Y}^{0}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}(\theta,z)dz\Big)\partial_{Y}\bar{u}+u^{\gamma}\partial_{\theta}\bar{u}-\partial_{YY}u^{\gamma}-\gamma\partial_{\theta\theta}u^{\gamma}\Big]u^{\gamma}d\theta dY

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\tilde{f}u^{\gamma}d\theta dY.

It’s easy to get

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big[-\partial_{YY}u^{\gamma}-\gamma\partial_{\theta\theta}u^{\gamma}\big]u^{\gamma}d\theta dY=\|\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+\gamma\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}.

Recall the estimates (
2.88
) and (
2.90
), we have

\displaystyle\big|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\partial^{k}_{Y}(\bar{u}-u_{e}(1))\big<Y\big>^{l}\big|\leq C(j,k,l)\eta,

\displaystyle\big|\partial^{j}_{\theta}\partial^{k}_{Y}(\bar{v}-v^{(1)}_{e}(\theta,1))\big<Y\big>^{l}\big|\leq C(j,k,l)\eta,\ \big|\partial^{j}_{\theta}v^{(1)}_{e}(\theta,1)\big|\leq C(j)\eta,

thus we can deduce that

\displaystyle-\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big[\bar{u}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}+\bar{v}\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}+\Big(\int_{Y}^{0}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}(\theta,z)dz\Big)\partial_{Y}\bar{u}+u^{\gamma}\partial_{\theta}\bar{u}\Big]u^{\gamma}d\theta dY+\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\tilde{f}u^{\gamma}d\theta dY

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{1}{2}\big[\partial_{\theta}\bar{u}+\partial_{Y}\bar{v}\big]\big(u^{\gamma}\big)^{2}d\theta dr+\|Y^{2}\bar{u}_{Y}\|_{\infty}\Big\|\frac{\int_{Y}^{0}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}dz}{Y}\Big\|_{2}\Big\|\frac{u^{\gamma}}{Y}\Big\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\|Y^{2}\bar{u}_{\theta}\|_{\infty}\Big\|\frac{u^{\gamma}}{Y}\Big\|_{2}^{2}+\|Y\tilde{f}\|_{2}\Big\|\frac{u^{\gamma}}{Y}\Big\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\eta\big[\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\big]+C\|Y\tilde{f}\|_{2}\|\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\|_{2},

where we use
\bar{u}_{\theta}+\bar{v}_{Y}=0
and the Hardy inequality

\Big\|\frac{\int_{Y}^{0}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}dz}{Y}\Big\|_{2}\leq C\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2},\ \Big\|\frac{u^{\gamma}}{Y}\Big\|_{2}\leq C\|\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}.

By collecting the above estimates, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+\gamma\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C\eta\big[\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\big]+C\|Y\tilde{f}\|_{2}\|\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\|_{2},

where
C
is independent on
\eta
and
\gamma
. If
\eta
is small enough, we deduce that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+\gamma\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C\eta\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+C\|Y\tilde{f}\|_{2}^{2}.

(2.109)

Next we multiply the first equation in (
2.4.3
) by
\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}
and integrate in
(\theta,r)\in(0,2\pi)\times(-\infty,0)
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big[\bar{u}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}+\bar{v}\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}+\Big(\int_{Y}^{0}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}(\theta,z)dz\Big)\partial_{Y}\bar{u}+u^{\gamma}\partial_{\theta}\bar{u}-\partial_{YY}u^{\gamma}-\gamma\partial_{\theta\theta}u^{\gamma}\Big]\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}d\theta dY

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\tilde{f}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}d\theta dY.

It’s direct to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bar{u}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}d\theta dY=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bar{u}\big|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\big|^{2}d\theta dY\geq(\alpha-C\eta)\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|^{2}_{2}.

The diffusion term can be computed as follows

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big[-\partial_{YY}u^{\gamma}-\gamma\partial_{\theta\theta}u^{\gamma}\big]\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}d\theta dY=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\theta}\big(\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\big)^{2}-\frac{\gamma}{2}\partial_{\theta}\big(\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\big)^{2}\Big)d\theta dY=0.

Moreover, there holds

\displaystyle-\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big[\bar{v}\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}+\Big(\int_{Y}^{0}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}(\theta,z)dz\Big)\partial_{Y}\bar{u}+u^{\gamma}\partial_{\theta}\bar{u}\Big]\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}d\theta dY+\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\tilde{f}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}d\theta dY

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\bar{v}\|_{\infty}\|\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}+\|Y\bar{u}_{Y}\|_{\infty}\Big\|\frac{\int_{Y}^{0}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}dz}{Y}\Big\|_{2}\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\|Y\partial_{\theta}\bar{u}\|_{\infty}\Big\|\frac{u^{\gamma}}{Y}\Big\|_{2}\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}+\|\tilde{f}\|_{2}\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\eta\big[\|\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|^{2}_{2}\big]+\|\tilde{f}\|_{2}\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}.

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\alpha\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C\eta\big[\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\big]+\|\tilde{f}\|_{2}\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}.

We then choose
\eta_{0}
small enough such that for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
, there holds

\displaystyle\alpha\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C\eta\big\|\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+C\|\tilde{f}\|_{2}^{2}.

(2.110)

It follows from (
2.109
) and (
2.110
) that we have

\displaystyle\alpha\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+\gamma\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C\|Y\tilde{f}\|_{2}^{2}+C\|\tilde{f}\|_{2}^{2}.

(2.111)

According to the first equation in (
2.4.3
), we deduce

\displaystyle\|\partial_{YY}u^{\gamma}\|^{2}_{2}+2\gamma\|\partial_{\theta Y}u^{\gamma}\|^{2}_{2}+\gamma^{2}\|\partial_{\theta\theta}u^{\gamma}\|^{2}_{2}=\Big\|\partial_{YY}u^{\gamma}+\gamma\partial_{\theta\theta}u^{\gamma}\Big\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg\|\bar{u}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}+\bar{v}\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}+\Big(\int_{Y}^{0}\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}(\theta,z)dz\Big)\partial_{Y}\bar{u}+u^{\gamma}\partial_{\theta}\bar{u}-\tilde{f}\bigg\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\big[\|\tilde{f}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}\big]\leq C\|Y\tilde{f}\|_{2}^{2}+C\|\tilde{f}\|_{2}^{2}.

(2.112)

Collecting the estimates (
2.111
) and (
2.4.3
), we obtain

\displaystyle\alpha\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\partial_{Y}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\partial_{YY}u^{\gamma}\|^{2}_{2}+\gamma\|\partial_{\theta}u^{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}+2\gamma\|\partial_{\theta Y}u^{\gamma}\|^{2}_{2}+\gamma^{2}\|\partial_{\theta\theta}u^{\gamma}\|^{2}_{2}\leq C\|\big<Y\big>\tilde{f}\|_{2}^{2}

which shows the existence and uniqueness of solution for system (
2.4.3
) for any
\gamma>0
in
\dot{H}^{1}_{0}
. Moreover the solution is smooth if
\tilde{f}
is smooth. Set

\displaystyle u:=\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}u^{\gamma},\ v:=\int_{Y}^{0}\partial_{\theta}u(\theta,d)dz,

then

\displaystyle\alpha\|\partial_{\theta}u\|_{2}^{2}+\|\partial_{Y}u\|_{2}^{2}+\|\partial_{YY}u\|^{2}_{2}\leq C\|\big<Y\big>\tilde{f}\|^{2}_{2}

(2.113)

and
(u,v)
solves the system (
2.4.3
) except the boundary condition
\lim\limits_{Y\rightarrow-\infty}\partial_{Y}u=0
.

Finally, we show that the derivatives of
u,v
decay fast as
Y\rightarrow-\infty
. Let
\psi=\int_{0}^{Y}\bar{u}(\theta,z)dz
and

\displaystyle\tilde{u}(\theta,\psi)=u(\theta,Y(\theta,\psi)),\ \tilde{v}(\theta,\psi)=v(\theta,Y(\theta,\psi)),\ F(\theta,\psi)=\frac{\tilde{f}(\theta,Y(\theta,\psi))}{\bar{u}(\theta,Y(\theta,\psi))},

then there holds

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{\theta}\tilde{u}-\partial_{\psi}(a(\theta,\psi)\partial_{\psi}\tilde{u})+b(\theta,\psi)\tilde{u}+c(\theta,\psi)\tilde{v}=F(\theta,\psi),\\[7.0pt]
\tilde{u}(\theta+2\pi,\psi)=\tilde{u}(\theta,\psi),\\[5.0pt]
\tilde{u}(\theta,0)=0,\ \lim_{\psi\rightarrow-\infty}\partial_{\psi}\tilde{u}(\theta,\psi)=0,\end{array}\right.

where

\displaystyle a(\theta,\psi)=\bar{u}(\theta,Y(\theta,\psi)),\ b(\theta,\psi)=\frac{\partial_{\theta}\bar{u}(\theta,Y(\theta,\psi))}{\bar{u}(\theta,Y(\theta,\psi))},\ c(\theta,\psi)=\frac{\partial_{Y}\bar{u}(\theta,Y(\theta,\psi))}{\bar{u}(\theta,Y(\theta,\psi))}.

Notice that there exist
\eta_{0}>0
such that for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
, there holds

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{2}\leq\bar{u}(\theta,Y)\leq\alpha,\ \forall(\theta,Y)\in[0,2\pi]\times(-\infty,0].

Thus, we deduce that
\frac{\alpha}{2}\leq\frac{|\psi|}{|Y|}\leq\alpha
.

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\theta}\tilde{u}\psi^{l}\|_{2}+\|\partial_{\psi}\tilde{u}_{\neq}\psi^{l}\|_{2}+\|\partial_{\psi\psi}\tilde{u}\psi^{l}\|_{2}\leq C(l)\|F\big<\psi\big>^{l+1}\|_{2},

(2.118)

where

\tilde{u}_{\neq}=\tilde{u}-u_{0}(\psi),\ u_{0}(\psi)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\tilde{u}(\theta,\psi)d\theta.

From (
2.113
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\theta}\tilde{u}\|_{2}+\|\partial_{\psi}\tilde{u}\|_{2}+\|\partial_{\psi\psi}\tilde{u}\|_{2}\leq C\|F\big<\psi\big>\|_{2},

thus (
2.118
) holds for
l=0
.

For any
l\geq 1
, multiplying
\tilde{u}_{\neq}\psi^{2l}
in (
2.4.3
) and integrating in
[0,2\pi]\times(-\infty,0]
, we obtain
